<a id="read:buffer-cost"></a>
Let $T_{J,N}$ be the sum of the sup norms of all terminal background slots whose spin supports meet the buffer or its interior. We may bound it by the larger sum over slots whose recorded carriers meet that region. There are $O(b^2)$ possible cells at which to root such a slot. The exponential weights pay the choices of animals and any distance of their anchors from those cells. The background $L^p$ bound from Proposition <a href="#flow:tuned" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flow:tuned">24</a> and Minkowski’s inequality consequently give $$\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left\lVert T_{J,N}\right\rVert_{L^p}
    \le C b^2s_J=O_D(J^{-9/50}).
 \label{read:buffer-cost}$$ Finite-box errors vanish by the preceding fixed-step argument. We have chosen the fixed background moment $p$ sufficiently large; any $p>25$ suffices for the probability estimates below, and the earlier construction allows much larger values.

Put $Q_J=w_{J,y_1}w_{J,y_2}$ and $q_J=\langle Q_J\rangle_0$, where the latter is plane reference expectation. If $T_{J,N}\le1$, delete these background slots. For a bounded observable and a perturbation $W$ with $\left\lVert W\right\rVert_\infty\le T$, differentiation of normalized expectations along $\exp(tW)$, $0\le t\le1$, gives $$\left|\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm dt}\langle Q\rangle_t\right|
   =|\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_t(Q,W)|\le2\left\lVert Q\right\rVert_\infty T.$$ Thus deletion changes the expectation of $Q_J$ by at most $CT_{J,N}$. Conditionally on spins outside the buffer, the remaining law inside is the reference Ising law with some boundary data. The product $Q_J$ is even and has bounded sup norm. Its support diameter in terminal units is bounded uniformly in <a href="#read:stop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:stop">[read:stop]</a>. The even conditional reference estimate of Proposition <a href="#ref:operator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ref:operator">3</a> therefore changes its expectation from the plane value by at most $C/b$, uniformly in those outer data. On the complementary event we use the uniform bound $C$ on both expectations. In particular, if $$B_{J,N}=\{T_{J,N}>J^{-7/50}\},
 \qquad
 \Delta_{J,N}=\langle Q_J\rangle_{J,N,\omega}-q_J,$$ then <a id="read:buffer-good"></a>$$|\Delta_{J,N}|\le C(J^{-7/50}+J^{-4/25})
     \quad\hbox{on }B_{J,N}^{\mathrm c},
 \qquad |\Delta_{J,N}|\le C,
 \qquad
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb P(B_{J,N})\le C_DJ^{-p/25}.
 \label{read:buffer-good}$$ The probability bound follows from Markov’s inequality and <a href="#read:buffer-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:buffer-cost">[read:buffer-cost]</a>; it requires no independence between $T_{J,N}$ and the amplitudes.

<a id="read:two-errors"></a>
Here are the distinct estimates needed for the first and second moments. On $B_{J,N}^{\mathrm c}$, <a href="#read:product-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:product-moments">[read:product-moments]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\bigl[|Z_J\Delta_{J,N}|\mathbf 1_{B_{J,N}^{\mathrm c}}\bigr]
     &\le J^{1/8-7/50+o(1)}=J^{-3/200+o(1)},\\
 \left\lVert Z_J\Delta_{J,N}\mathbf 1_{B_{J,N}^{\mathrm c}}\right\rVert_{L^2}
     &\le J^{1/8-7/50+o(1)}.
\end{aligned}$$ On the exceptional event, Hölder’s inequality gives, after the finite-volume limit, $$\begin{aligned}
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \mathbb E\bigl[|Z_J\Delta_{J,N}|\mathbf 1_{B_{J,N}}\bigr]
     &\le C\left\lVert Z_J\right\rVert_{L^2}J^{-p/50}
       =J^{1/8-p/50+o(1)},\\
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \left\lVert Z_J\Delta_{J,N}\mathbf 1_{B_{J,N}}\right\rVert_{L^2}
     &\le C\left\lVert Z_J\right\rVert_{L^4}J^{-p/100}
       =J^{3/8-p/100+o(1)}.
\end{aligned}$$ The first error tends to zero. For some fixed $\eta>0$ the second is $O(J^{1/8-\eta+o(1)})$, since $p>25$. Together with <a href="#read:amplitude-readout" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:amplitude-readout">[read:amplitude-readout]</a>, these estimates prove $$\begin{split}
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \mathbb E\left|r_J^{2d}
  \langle\sigma_{y_1}\sigma_{y_2}\rangle^{\mathrm{free}}_{N,\omega}
       -q_JZ_J\right|&=o(1),\\
 \limsup_{N\to\infty}
 \left\lVert r_J^{2d}
  \langle\sigma_{y_1}\sigma_{y_2}\rangle^{\mathrm{free}}_{N,\omega}
       -q_JZ_J\right\rVert_{L^2}
       &=O(J^{1/8-\eta+o(1)}).
 \end{split}
 \label{read:two-errors}$$ Decrease $\eta$ if necessary to include the joint-source error $J^{-1/8+o(1)}$. The separate $L^1$ estimate is useful: a relative $L^2$ estimate alone would not recover the bounded first moment.

<a id="read:reference-pair"></a>
The two reference averaging squares defining $w_{J,y_a}$ are disjoint. Conditional independence in the reference model, given their contours, and the tower property yield the exact equality $$q_J=r_J^{2d}\langle\sigma_{y_1}\sigma_{y_2}\rangle_0.
 \label{read:reference-pair}$$ The separated reference correlation limit, uniformly over the compact window <a href="#read:stop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:stop">[read:stop]</a> and over directions, gives $0<c\le q_J\le C$. Thus arbitrary integer separations and grid-cell positions are covered.

<a id="stmt-read-moments"></a>
<div id="read:moments" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31** (Disorder moments at tuning). *Let $C_{*,\omega}(y_1,y_2)$ be the free infinite-volume spin correlation at $t_*(D)$, and let $J$ be determined by <a href="#read:stop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:stop">[read:stop]</a>. For every fixed sufficiently small $D>0$, uniformly in the two marks as their separation tends to infinity, <a id="read:moment-conclusion"></a>$$r_J^{2d}\mathbb EC_{*,\omega}(y_1,y_2)\asymp1,
 \qquad
 r_J^{4d}\mathbb EC_{*,\omega}(y_1,y_2)^2
       =J^{1/4+o(1)}.
 \label{read:moment-conclusion}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-read-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-read-moments</summary>

*Proof.* For each fixed separation, free ferromagnetic correlations increase as the volume increases and remain in $[0,1]$. Their limit exists, and bounded convergence passes both disorder moments to the limit. Here $J$ and $r_J$ are fixed during this passage. The first line of <a href="#read:two-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:two-errors">[read:two-errors]</a>, <a href="#read:product-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:product-moments">[read:product-moments]</a>, and <a href="#read:reference-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:reference-pair">[read:reference-pair]</a> give the first assertion, including a positive lower bound. For the second, the triangle inequality and its reverse in $L^2$ give $$\left|\left\lVert r_J^{2d}C_{*,\omega}(y_1,y_2)\right\rVert_{L^2}
             -q_J\left\lVert Z_J\right\rVert_{L^2}\right|
 \le O(J^{1/8-\eta+o(1)}).$$ The main term is $J^{1/8+o(1)}$, so squaring proves the second assertion. All finite-volume identities hold for each choice of seeds, whereas the original correlation depends only on the bonds. Averaging over the seeds thus leaves the asserted disorder moments unchanged. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### A finite-box estimate with wired boundary

The identification of the transition also needs an estimate in boxes whose boundary is fixed to plus. Such a boundary can create an odd saved scalar, so it is important to keep the carrier of that scalar until its distance from the central mark has been used.

<a id="stmt-read-arm"></a>
<div id="read:arm" class="proposition">

<a id="read:arm-bound"></a>
**Proposition 32** (Wired arm at tuning). *For any square box of radius $n$ centered at a lattice site $y$, let $\phi^w_{n,*,\omega}$ be the FK–Ising law at $t_*(D)$ with wired boundary. Then $$\mathbb E\left[\phi^w_{n,*,\omega}
    (y\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n(y))^{400}\right]
       \le n^{-400/8+o(1)}.
 \label{read:arm-bound}$$ The estimate is uniform in $y$. It also holds if the fixed wall is one lattice layer inside or outside the indicated square, including the convention with bonds crossing to an exterior wired layer.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-read-arm"><summary>Proof of stmt-read-arm</summary>

*Proof.* Under the Edwards–Sokal coupling <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[EdwardsSokal1988](#ref-EdwardsSokal1988)], the wired arm probability equals the spin expectation with the corresponding plus wall. Start a single source at $y$ and let $J_n$ be the largest integer satisfying <a id="read:arm-stop"></a>$$r_{J_n}\le n/(\log n)^2.
 \label{read:arm-stop}$$ Thus $r_{J_n}\asymp n/(\log n)^2$, $J_n=O(\log n)$, and the principal halo remains far from the wall for every $j\le J_n$.

Use the finite-box blocking rule, which forbids cuts near the wall and leaves those interactions in the lists. Every singleton term whose full footprint stays in the bulk equals its plane counterpart. By the weighted $L^{400}$ source estimate and Proposition <a href="#mark:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mark:moments">28</a>, the sum of the sup costs of these bulk terms at the final scale has $L^{400}$ norm bounded by a fixed power of $1+J_n$. Indeed $A_{j,400}^{1/400}=j^{399/16+o(1)}$, and summing the exponential slot weights only changes its constant. A local odd scalar is zero: before the boundary spins are evaluated, the bulk kernels and the plane centering are invariant under simultaneous spin flip.

<a id="read:boundary-tail"></a>
It remains to estimate all terms that can see the wall, including saved scalar increments. Every such source footprint contains the mark and reaches the boundary region. At scale $j$ its carrier length is therefore at least $c n/r_j$. The deterministic weighted source transport costs a fixed factor $K$ per step, even with arbitrary principal background sizes: selected averages are normalized, retained large terms do not enlarge a source norm, and the small-field coefficient sums are bounded. This is the source estimate of Section <a href="#sec:norms" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:norms">4</a> applied without averaging over the randomness. Keep an additional exponential carrier weight. At each possible scale of first boundary contact it supplies $\exp(-c'n/r_j)$. Sum these contributions, retaining the source rescaling up to $J_n$; changing $K$ absorbs both that rescaling and the sum over the possible first-contact steps. The total deterministic cost at the stopping scale is at most $$C K^{J_n}\exp(-c'n/r_{J_n})
 \le \exp\bigl(C\log n-c''(\log n)^2\bigr).
 \label{read:boundary-tail}$$ One can equivalently subtract the plane and finite-box constructions: the first coefficient where they differ must have a footprint meeting the wall, and each later difference is transported with the same weighted estimate. This verifies the bound for all nonbulk coefficients without assuming that their laws agree with plane laws. For an odd scalar the carrier weight is used before its support is forgotten, so <a href="#read:boundary-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:boundary-tail">[read:boundary-tail]</a> includes all nonzero scalar contributions. Initial fixed constants merely change $C$.

The exact normalized one-source identity now expresses the plus magnetization, multiplied by $r_{J_n}^d$, as the terminal expectation of the scaled source plus its saved scalar. Its absolute value is bounded by the total sup costs just estimated, irrespective of the terminal background. Hence, for some fixed $M<\infty$, $$\left\lVert\langle\sigma_y\rangle^+_{n,*,\omega}\right\rVert_{L^{400}}
 \le r_{J_n}^{-d}\left(C(1+J_n)^M
                  +C K^{J_n}e^{-c'n/r_{J_n}}\right)
       =n^{-d+o(1)}.$$ Raising this inequality to the power $400$ proves <a href="#read:arm-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:arm-bound">[read:arm-bound]</a>. Translating the box changes none of the constants because the source estimates are uniform in its mark. Moving the wall by a bounded number of lattice layers changes the lower bound on the boundary-reaching carrier length only by a fixed factor, proving the remaining conventions. ◻

</details>

In particular the averaged plus magnetization vanishes at tuning. The next section uses the stronger finite-box bound <a href="#read:arm-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="read:arm-bound">[read:arm-bound]</a> to identify the tuned parameter and to show that magnetization becomes positive above it.

<a id="sec:transition"></a>
### Identification of the physical transition

The construction in Proposition <a href="#flow:tuned" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flow:tuned">24</a> selects a parameter $t_*=t_*(D)$, and Propositions <a href="#read:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:moments">31</a> and <a href="#read:arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:arm">32</a> give correlation moments and a wired one-arm estimate there. We now show that $t_*=0$ and that this value is the magnetization threshold. The first assertion uses partition functions on tori and a product-space influence inequality. The second uses the high moment in Proposition <a href="#read:arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:arm">32</a> to control a decision-tree exploration in a fixed environment.

<a id="crit:fk-law"></a>
<a id="crit:magnetization"></a>
For a finite graph $G=(V,E)$, put $v_e=e^{2K_e}-1$ and write $$\label{crit:fk-law}
 \phi_{G,t,\omega}(A)
 =\frac{1}{\mathcal Z_{G,t,\omega}}
       2^{k(A)}\prod_{e\in A}v_e,
 \qquad A\subset E.$$ Here $k(A)$ counts components of the spanning subgraph $(V,A)$; a specified boundary wiring is imposed before counting components. Superscripts $\mathrm f$ and $\mathrm w$ indicate free and wired boundaries. All these probabilities are normalized at fixed $\omega$. The Edwards–Sokal coupling <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[EdwardsSokal1988](#ref-EdwardsSokal1988)] identifies the wired connection probability to the boundary with the corresponding plus-boundary spin expectation. In infinite volume it gives $$\label{crit:magnetization}
 \langle\sigma_0\rangle^+_{t,\omega}
       =\phi^{\mathrm w}_{t,\omega}(0\longleftrightarrow\infty).$$ Finite-energy bounds, positive association, and comparison in the couplings hold uniformly on any fixed compact $(t,D)$ window considered below. In particular, for some $\eta>0$, every one-edge conditional open probability, with any other edges prescribed, lies in $[\eta,1-\eta]$. This follows directly from <a href="#crit:fk-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:fk-law">[crit:fk-law]</a>: that probability is either $v_e/(1+v_e)$ or $v_e/(2+v_e)$.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Torus duality and twisted partition functions

Let $\mathbb T_\ell=(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z)^2$ have its usual square cellulation. For $A\subset E(\mathbb T_\ell)$, let $H(A)\subset H_1(\mathbb T_\ell;\mathbb F_2)=\mathbb F_2^2$ be the image of its cycle space, and put $r(A)=\dim H(A)$. The complementary dual configuration $A^*$ contains the dual of each edge outside $A$. For each nonzero character $\chi:\mathbb F_2^2\longrightarrow\mathbb F_2$, define the increasing, translation-invariant event $$\mathcal H_\chi=\{A:\chi|_{H(A)}\ne0\}.$$ Thus $\mathcal H_\chi$ asks for an open cycle detected by $\chi$.

<a id="stmt-crit-torus-duality"></a>
<div id="crit:torus-duality" class="lemma">

<a id="crit:homology-annihilator"></a>
**Lemma 33** (Torus duality). *Under the intersection pairing, $$\label{crit:homology-annihilator}
 H(A^*)=H(A)^\perp,\qquad r(A^*)=2-r(A).$$ If $v_{e^*}^*=2/v_e$, then complement duality carries the torus FK law at $v$ to a measure whose density with respect to the torus FK law at $v^*$ lies between $1/4$ and $4$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-torus-duality"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-torus-duality</summary>

<a id="crit:euler"></a>
*Proof.* The cycle space of $(V,A)$ has dimension $|A|-|V|+k(A)$. A cellular boundary is supported on $A$ precisely when its face coefficients are constant on each complementary dual component. The space of such boundaries therefore has dimension $k(A^*)-1$: its face coefficients have $k(A^*)$ degrees of freedom, and adding the constant two-chain does not change the boundary. Consequently $$\label{crit:euler}
 |A|-|V|+k(A)=k(A^*)-1+r(A).$$ Apply the same identity on the dual cellulation and add the two identities. The torus Euler formula $|V|-|E|+|F|=0$ gives $r(A)+r(A^*)=2$. Primal and complementary dual cycles have zero intersection, since a primal edge and its transverse dual cannot both be present. This inclusion, together with the dimension identity, proves <a href="#crit:homology-annihilator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:homology-annihilator">[crit:homology-annihilator]</a>.

The ratio of the two unnormalized weights is $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{2^{k(A)}\prod_{e\in A}v_e}
 {2^{k(A^*)}\prod_{e\notin A}(2/v_e)}
 &=\Big(\prod_{e\in E}v_e\Big)
            2^{k(A)-k(A^*)-|E|+|A|}\\
 &=\Big(\prod_{e\in E}v_e\Big)
            2^{|V|-|E|-1}\,2^{r(A)},
\end{aligned}$$ where the last equality uses <a href="#crit:euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:euler">[crit:euler]</a>. The first factor is independent of $A$, and $1\le2^{r(A)}\le4$. Normalization proves the claimed comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="crit:twist-identity"></a>
Choose signs $s_e^\chi\in\{-1,1\}$ representing the cocycle $\chi$, and let $Z^\chi(K)$ be the Ising partition function with interactions $K_es_e^\chi\sigma_x\sigma_y$. Write $Z^0(K)$ for the ordinary partition function. Expanding $$e^{K_es_e^\chi\sigma_x\sigma_y}
 =e^{-K_e}\bigl(1+v_e\mathbf 1_{\{\sigma_x=s_e^\chi\sigma_y\}}\bigr)$$ shows that a component of $A$ contributes two compatible spin assignments exactly when the product of seam signs around each of its cycles is $1$. An incompatible component contributes zero. Hence, for every environment, not only after averaging, $$\label{crit:twist-identity}
 \frac{Z^\chi(K)}{Z^0(K)}
          =\phi_{\mathbb T_\ell,t,\omega}(\mathcal H_\chi^c).$$

<a id="crit:reference-homology"></a>
At the critical reference couplings there is $a_0>0$, independent of $\ell$, such that $$\label{crit:reference-homology}
 a_0\le\phi^{\mathrm{ref}}_{\mathbb T_\ell}(\mathcal H_\chi)
                    \le1-a_0
 \qquad(\chi\ne0).$$ For completeness, choose a coordinate generator on which $\chi$ is nonzero. In a narrow toric strip along that generator, a bounded number of overlapping fixed-aspect rectangle crossings, with transverse crossings in their overlaps, produces an open generator cycle. The arbitrary-boundary crossing bounds of <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[CDH2016](#ref-CDH2016), Theorem 1.1], applied in the rectangular charts, and FK positive association give a uniform lower bound. Make both generator cycles in the dual configuration in the same way. Their homology has rank two, so <a href="#crit:homology-annihilator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:homology-annihilator">[crit:homology-annihilator]</a> forces $H(A)=0$. Lemma <a href="#crit:torus-duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:torus-duality">33</a> compares the complemented law with the critical dual FK law by an absolute factor. This proves the upper bound in <a href="#crit:reference-homology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:reference-homology">[crit:reference-homology]</a>. Only finitely many crossings are used, so their lower bounds do not deteriorate with $\ell$.

<a id="stmt-crit-torus-transfer"></a>
<div id="crit:torus-transfer" class="lemma">

<a id="crit:tuned-homology"></a>
**Lemma 34** (Transfer of torus nondegeneracy). *Fix a sufficiently large integer $Q$. After choosing the smallness constants of the construction sufficiently small, there is $a_1>0$ such that, for $\ell_J=Qr_J$ and every nonzero $\chi$, $$\label{crit:tuned-homology}
 a_1\le \mathbb E\phi_{\mathbb T_{\ell_J},t_*,\omega}(\mathcal H_\chi)
                 \le1-a_1$$ for all sufficiently large $J$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-torus-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-torus-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Apply the exact finite-volume map of Proposition <a href="#block:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="block:finite">12</a> to the untwisted model and to the $\chi$-twisted model, using the corresponding critical reference partition function in each case and the same decision seeds. A twist is removed on a contractible chart by a spin gauge transformation. Every background function is even, so the ambiguity of a simultaneous sign change on the chart does not alter it. Conditional reference averaging, the local decisions, and plane centering in this gauge therefore give identical chart coefficients and identical saved scalar increments in the two runs.

Here is why the errors outside charts remain uniform in $J$. Choose $Q$ larger than the fixed halos and chart margins in the construction. A principal output uses only a short parent footprint, by Lemma <a href="#block:carrier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="block:carrier">7</a>. That footprint lifts to the plane at every scale up to $J$, and its environment and seeds have the planar law. A nonchart footprint has length at least $cm$ when the torus has side $m$ at the scale carrying that footprint. Changing between input and output cells changes this constant by the fixed factor $L$ only. It cannot satisfy the short-footprint rule for a principal output. This also proves that a seam crossing a short footprint causes no exception: it is removed by the local gauge, whereas a genuinely nonchart input remains in the tail.

Keep the scalar supports until applying the enhanced tree weights in Lemma <a href="#block:tree" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="block:tree">9</a> and Corollary <a href="#block:diameter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="block:diameter">10</a>. Summing the possible nonchart footprints, including the neighborhoods used by the decisions, bounds the uncancelled scalar increment at this step by $$C_L\delta m^2e^{-cm}.$$ There are $O(m^2)$ possible roots; the additional length weight pays $e^{-cm}$ after the animal sum. The side lengths $m$ decrease geometrically and remain at least $Q$, so their total is bounded by $C_L\delta Q^2e^{-c'Q}$, independently of $J$. The final tail functions have total supremum norm at most $C\delta Q^2$. These estimates use the finite-volume weighted bounds before forgetting supports, and apply also to normalized conditional operations on nonchart terms.

To see precisely how these errors enter the desired ratio, let $c_J^\chi,H_J^\chi$ denote the accumulated scalar and terminal background for a twist, and use superscript $0$ for the untwisted run. Exact partition preservation gives <a id="crit:terminal-ratio"></a>$$\label{crit:terminal-ratio}
 \frac{Z^\chi(K)}{Z^0(K)}
 =\frac{Z^\chi(K^{\mathrm{ref}})}{Z^0(K^{\mathrm{ref}})}
       e^{c_J^\chi-c_J^0}
       \frac{\langle e^{H_J^\chi}\rangle_{J,\mathrm{ref},\chi}}
            {\langle e^{H_J^0}\rangle_{J,\mathrm{ref},0}}.$$ The brackets are normalized terminal reference expectations in the respective exact partition identities. Chart scalar increments cancel in $c_J^\chi-c_J^0$, leaving the nonchart bound above. At the final scale there are only $O(Q^2)$ principal slots. Their laws agree in charts with the planar laws, so the high-moment bounds along the tuned flow imply that their total supremum norm converges to zero in probability as $J\to\infty$. Denote this total, in the two runs together, by $P_J$. Exact partition preservation and $|\log\langle e^H\rangle|\le\left\lVert H\right\rVert_\infty$ now give <a id="crit:log-ratio-transfer"></a>$$\label{crit:log-ratio-transfer}
 \left|\log\frac{Z^\chi(K)/Z^0(K)}
                         {Z^\chi(K^{\mathrm{ref}})/Z^0(K^{\mathrm{ref}})}
       \right|
 \le P_J+C\delta Q^2+C_L\delta Q^2e^{-c'Q},
 \qquad P_J\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.$$ The reference ratios here are on the same microscopic torus; exact blocking has not replaced them by a limiting torus ratio. First fix $Q$, then choose $\delta$ and the permitted disorder so that the deterministic errors are smaller than a fixed tolerance depending on $a_0$, and finally let $J$ grow. By <a href="#crit:twist-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:twist-identity">[crit:twist-identity]</a> and <a href="#crit:reference-homology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:reference-homology">[crit:reference-homology]</a>, on an event of probability tending to one all three tuned character probabilities lie in a common compact subinterval of $(0,1)$. Their values always lie in $[0,1]$, so averaging proves <a href="#crit:tuned-homology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:tuned-homology">[crit:tuned-homology]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### Sharp thresholds after averaging the environment

We next prove the threshold estimate needed for the torus events. The environment is independent across bonds, but the FK edges are not. A monotone sequential representation lets us apply the product influence theorem while keeping the useful derivative as a quenched covariance.

<a id="stmt-crit-threshold"></a>
<div id="crit:threshold" class="lemma">

<a id="crit:threshold-inequality"></a>
**Lemma 35** (Torus threshold inequality). *Fix a compact parameter window containing $0$ and $t_*(D)$, with uniformly positive, bounded couplings. If $\mathcal A$ is any increasing translation-invariant event on $\mathbb T_\ell$ and $f_\ell(t)=\mathbb E\phi_{\mathbb T_\ell,t,\omega}(\mathcal A)$, then $$\label{crit:threshold-inequality}
 f_\ell'(t)\ge c\log\ell\, f_\ell(t)(1-f_\ell(t)),
 \qquad \ell\ge3,$$ with $c>0$ independent of $\ell$ and $\mathcal A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-threshold"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-threshold</summary>

<a id="crit:derivative"></a>
*Proof.* For a fixed environment abbreviate its FK law to $\phi$, and set $X_e=\mathbf 1_{\{e\in A\}}$ and $c_e(\omega)=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\phi_{t,\omega}}(\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A},X_e)\ge0$. Differentiating the finite sum in <a href="#crit:fk-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:fk-law">[crit:fk-law]</a> gives $$\label{crit:derivative}
 f_\ell'(t)=\sum_e\mathbb E[\lambda_e(t,\omega)c_e(\omega)],
 \qquad
 \lambda_e=\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm dt}\log v_e.$$ Since $\,\mathrm dK_e/\,\mathrm dt=\tfrac12\tanh(2K_e)$, the coefficients $\lambda_e=\tanh(2K_e)e^{2K_e}/(e^{2K_e}-1)$ have uniform positive lower and upper bounds.

Represent each environment bit by a uniform variable $V_e$, with $\xi_e=1$ when $V_e\ge1/2$. Order the FK edges arbitrarily as $e_1,\ldots,e_M$ and use further independent uniforms $U_i$ to set $$X_{e_i}=\mathbf 1_{\{U_i\ge1-p_i\}},\qquad
 p_i=\phi_{t,\omega}(X_{e_i}=1\mid X_{e_1},\ldots,X_{e_{i-1}}).$$ The conditional FK laws are increasing both in the prescribed past and in the couplings. Induction in $i$ therefore makes the entire sample increasing in all the $U_i$ and $V_e$. Let $F$ be the Boolean indicator of $\mathcal A$ in this product representation. For one uniform coordinate $z$, its influence $I_z$ is the probability, over all other coordinates, that the section of $F$ in $z$ has values zero and one on sets of positive Lebesgue measure.

We first bound an FK-uniform influence. Fix the environment and let $P=(X_{e_1},\ldots,X_{e_{i-1}})$ be the past. Write $$p(P)=\phi(X_{e_i}=1\mid P),\qquad
 a_b(P)=\phi(\mathcal A\mid P,X_{e_i}=b),\quad b=0,1.$$ The future uniforms couple the two branches monotonically. As both intervals for $X_{e_i}$ have length at least $\eta$, the section in $U_i$ is nonconstant exactly when the two resulting Boolean outputs differ. Averaging those future uniforms gives $a_1(P)-a_0(P)$. Consequently <a id="crit:uniform-influence"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 I_{U_i}
 &=\mathbb E\,\phi\bigl(a_1(P)-a_0(P)\bigr)\nonumber\\
 &\le\frac{1}{\eta(1-\eta)}
       \mathbb E\,\phi\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_\phi(\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A},X_{e_i}\mid P)\bigr)
 \le C\mathbb Ec_{e_i}(\omega).\label{crit:uniform-influence}
\end{aligned}$$ For the last inequality, total covariance gives an additional term $$\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_\phi\bigl(\phi(\mathcal A\mid P),
                        \phi(X_{e_i}=1\mid P)\bigr)\ge0.$$ Both functions are increasing in $P$, and the marginal distribution of $P$ is positively associated. This proves the indicated sign.

For the environment coordinate $V_e$, condition on every other bond. Let $\phi_-$ and $\phi_+$ be the FK laws with its low and high value, and put $R=v_e^+/v_e^-\ge1$. Changing this bond multiplies the weight by $R^{X_e}$, so direct normalization gives <a id="crit:bond-tilt"></a>$$\label{crit:bond-tilt}
 \phi_+(\mathcal A)-\phi_-(\mathcal A)
 =\frac{(R-1)\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\phi_-}(\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A},X_e)}
              {1+(R-1)\phi_-(X_e)}.$$ Monotonicity of the common-uniform coupling makes the left side the conditional influence of $V_e$. The ratio $R$ is uniformly bounded. After averaging the other bonds, the low-bond covariance is at most twice $\mathbb Ec_e(\omega)$, since both low- and high-bond covariances are nonnegative. Thus $I_{V_e}\le C\mathbb Ec_e(\omega)$ as well.

<a id="crit:max-influence"></a>
Translation invariance of the averaged law and of $\mathcal A$ makes $\mathbb Ec_e(\omega)$ constant within each of the two edge orientations. Each orientation has $\ell^2$ edges. Equation <a href="#crit:derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:derivative">[crit:derivative]</a> therefore implies $$\label{crit:max-influence}
 \max_z I_z\le C\ell^{-2}f_\ell'(t).$$ There is no symmetry requirement on the chosen ordering or the sequential sampler: only the averaged covariances were compared by translation. There are $N=2M=4\ell^2$ product coordinates. The BKKKL inequality <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BKKKL1992](#ref-BKKKL1992)], in the null-set-invariant formulation <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[GJN2013](#ref-GJN2013), Theorem 2.3 and Equation (2.3)], gives $$\max_z I_z\ge c f_\ell(t)(1-f_\ell(t))\frac{\log N}{N}.$$ Combine this with <a href="#crit:max-influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:max-influence">[crit:max-influence]</a> to obtain <a href="#crit:threshold-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:threshold-inequality">[crit:threshold-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-crit-tuning-zero"></a>
<div id="crit:tuning-zero" class="proposition">

**Proposition 36**. *For every sufficiently small fixed $D>0$, the tuned parameter is $t_*(D)=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-tuning-zero"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-tuning-zero</summary>

*Proof.* The dual transformation $v_{e^*}^*=2/v_e$ is equivalent to $\sinh(2K_e)\sinh(2K_{e^*}^*)=1$. Thus, in the coordinates <a href="#eq:model-td" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-td">[eq:model-td]</a>, it sends $(t,D,\xi)$ to $(-t,D,-\xi)$ on the dual lattice. At $t=0$ the environment law is invariant. Write $a_i=\mathbb E\phi_{\mathbb T_\ell,0,\omega}(r(A)=i)$. Lemma <a href="#crit:torus-duality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:torus-duality">33</a> gives $a_0/4\le a_2\le4a_0$. In particular, $a_1+a_2\ge1/5$ and $a_0+a_1\ge1/5$.

There are three nonzero characters of $\mathbb F_2^2$. A one-dimensional homology space is detected by two of them, whereas a two-dimensional space is detected by all three. Therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\chi\ne0}\mathbb E\phi_{0,\omega}(\mathcal H_\chi)
      &=2a_1+3a_2,\\
 \sum_{\chi\ne0}\mathbb E\phi_{0,\omega}(\mathcal H_\chi^c)
      &=3a_0+a_1.
\end{aligned}$$ It follows that at $t=0$ at least one character has probability bounded below by an absolute positive constant, and at least one has probability bounded away from one. The two characters need not be the same.

Apply Lemma <a href="#crit:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:threshold">35</a> to each $\mathcal H_\chi$ along $\ell_J=Qr_J$. On $(0,1)$ it states $$\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm dt}\log\frac{f_{\ell_J}(t)}{1-f_{\ell_J}(t)}
                         \ge c\log\ell_J.$$ If $t_*>0$, integrate from $0$ to $t_*$ and use the upper bound in <a href="#crit:tuned-homology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:tuned-homology">[crit:tuned-homology]</a>. Every character probability at zero would tend to zero. If $t_*<0$, integrate from $t_*$ to zero and use the lower bound in <a href="#crit:tuned-homology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:tuned-homology">[crit:tuned-homology]</a>; every character probability at zero would tend to one. Both conclusions contradict the preceding absolute bounds. Hence $t_*=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Arm decay below zero and percolation above zero

Proposition <a href="#read:arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:arm">32</a> and Proposition <a href="#crit:tuning-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:tuning-zero">36</a> already imply zero plus magnetization at $t=0$. The next argument extracts a stronger consequence strictly below zero. It applies the monotone OSSS inequality in each good environment, so it does not require a sharpness theorem for a translation-invariant deterministic coupling field.

<a id="stmt-crit-subcritical-arm"></a>
<div id="crit:subcritical-arm" class="lemma">

<a id="crit:subcritical-decay"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Averaged subcritical arm bound). *For every fixed $t'<0$ in the parameter window and sufficiently small fixed $D>0$, $$\label{crit:subcritical-decay}
 \mathbb E\phi^{\mathrm w}_{t',\omega}
                  (0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)
                      \le C_{t',D}n^{-6}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-subcritical-arm"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-subcritical-arm</summary>

<a id="crit:exception"></a>
*Proof.* Put $m=\lfloor n^{1/2}\rfloor$ and, for $z\in\Lambda_{2n}$, define $$q_z(\omega)=\phi^{\mathrm w}_{z+\Lambda_m,0,\omega}
           (z\longleftrightarrow z+\partial\Lambda_m).$$ Use the wall convention of Proposition <a href="#read:arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:arm">32</a>; shifting the wall one lattice step does not affect its uniform estimate. Let $\mathcal G_n=\{q_z\le n^{-1/50}\text{ for every }z\in\Lambda_{2n}\}$. Markov’s inequality, the $400$th moment in Proposition <a href="#read:arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:arm">32</a>, and a union bound give $$\begin{aligned}
\label{crit:exception}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)
 &\le Cn^2 n^{400/50}m^{-400/8+o(1)}
   =n^{-15+o(1)}\le Cn^{-6}.
\end{aligned}$$ All boxes use the same global environment; independence of these box events is unnecessary. On $\mathcal G_n$, the same inequalities hold throughout $t\in[t',0]$ by monotonicity in $t$.

Fix such an environment, and consider $$f_\omega(t)=\phi^{\mathrm w}_{\Lambda_{4n},t,\omega}(\mathcal A_n),
 \qquad
 \mathcal A_n=\{0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n
                        \text{ by a path in }\Lambda_n\}.$$ Choose $k$ uniformly from $\{1,\ldots,n\}$, independently of the FK configuration. Explore all open components in $\Lambda_n$ meeting its square shell $\partial\Lambda_k$, revealing all edges of the induced graph on $\Lambda_n$ incident to the vertices reached. Retain their actual connectivity; the shell vertices are not identified with one another. Every path witnessing $\mathcal A_n$ meets the chosen shell, and its whole open component is explored. Thus this algorithm determines $\mathcal A_n$.

For an edge $e=xy$ within $\Lambda_n$, the chance that the chosen shell lies within distance $m+2$ of $x$ or $y$ is at most $Cm/n$. Otherwise, querying $e$ requires that one endpoint is already joined to the shell by a revealed open path, and hence has an open arm of radius $m$. The endpoint box is contained in $\Lambda_{4n}$. Conditional on all edges outside that box, the domain Markov property gives an induced boundary partition, dominated by full wiring. Its conditional arm probability is consequently at most $q_x$ or $q_y$, uniformly for $t\in[t',0]$. The revealment $\delta_e(t)$ therefore satisfies <a id="crit:revealment"></a>$$\label{crit:revealment}
 \max_e\delta_e(t)
          \le C(n^{-1/2}+n^{-1/50})\le Cn^{-1/50}.$$ Edges outside $\Lambda_n$ are never queried.

The monotone-measure OSSS inequality <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[DCRT2019](#ref-DCRT2019), Theorem 1.1], averaged over the independent choice of shell, gives $$f_\omega(t)(1-f_\omega(t))
 \le\sum_e\delta_e(t)
       \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\phi^{\mathrm w}_{\Lambda_{4n},t,\omega}}
                         (\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A_n},X_e).$$ The shell construction is the exploration underlying <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[DCRT2019](#ref-DCRT2019), Lemma 3.2]; here its revealment is bounded using the quenched local arms on $\mathcal G_n$. Differentiating the FK weights as in <a href="#crit:derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:derivative">[crit:derivative]</a> and using their positive lower derivative bound yields $$f_\omega'(t)\ge c n^{1/50}f_\omega(t)(1-f_\omega(t)).$$ The event that all four edges incident to zero are closed has probability at least $\eta^4$, by sequential conditioning and finite energy. Hence $f_\omega(0)\le1-\eta^4$. Integration of the preceding logit inequality gives $$f_\omega(t')\le C\exp\{-c|t'|n^{1/50}\}.$$ After averaging, <a href="#crit:exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:exception">[crit:exception]</a> contributes at most $Cn^{-6}$. Finally, the restriction of the infinite wired law to this cylinder event is dominated by the wired law in $\Lambda_{4n}$, by domain comparison. This proves <a href="#crit:subcritical-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:subcritical-decay">[crit:subcritical-decay]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-crit-identification"></a>
<div id="crit:identification" class="proposition">

**Proposition 38** (Transition in the $(t,D)$ coordinates). *For every sufficiently small fixed $D>0$, the disorder-averaged plus magnetization vanishes at $t\le0$ and is positive at every $t>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-identification"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-identification</summary>

*Proof.* At zero, Proposition <a href="#read:arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:arm">32</a> implies $\mathbb E\phi^{\mathrm w}_{\Lambda_n,0,\omega}
 (0\longleftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)\to0$ by Hölder’s inequality. Equations <a href="#crit:magnetization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:magnetization">[crit:magnetization]</a> and monotonicity give zero magnetization for $t\le0$.

Fix $s>0$ small enough that $-s$ lies in the parameter window. Planar free/wired duality sends the infinite free FK law at $(s,D,\xi)$ to the infinite wired dual law at $(-s,D,-\xi)$. This identity follows first from the finite planar bond expansion and then by taking free and wired limits; see also the boundary conventions in <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[CDH2016](#ref-CDH2016), Section 2.5]. The dual environment still has the same independent binary law. Failure of a primal crossing of a fixed-aspect rectangle of diameter comparable to $n$ entails a dual crossing in the transverse direction, and hence a dual arm of length at least $cn$ from a vertex in that rectangle. There are $O(n^2)$ possible vertices. Lemma <a href="#crit:subcritical-arm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:subcritical-arm">37</a>, translated to each such vertex and applied at $-s$, therefore bounds the averaged failure probability by $Cn^{-4}$.

For each $n=2^kn_0$, use the four rectangular sides of $\Lambda_{2n}\setminus\Lambda_n$, each of width $n$ and length $4n$. Their longitudinal crossings intersect in the corner squares and contain an open circuit surrounding $\Lambda_n$. Require also a left–right crossing of $[n/2,5n]\times[-n/4,n/4]$. Its initial point lies inside $\Lambda_n$ and its endpoint lies outside $\Lambda_{4n}$, so it must intersect both this circuit and the circuit at scale $2n$. Rounding rectangle endpoints by a lattice step is harmless; one may choose $n_0$ divisible by four. All aspect ratios are fixed and there are five requested crossings per scale. The probability that any of them fails is at most $$C\sum_{k\ge0}(2^kn_0)^{-4},$$ which is smaller than one for large $n_0$. On the complementary event the joined circuits form an infinite open connected set. Thus the joint law obtained by first sampling the environment and then its infinite free FK configuration has positive probability of an infinite cluster. This joint law is translation stationary. If the origin had zero probability of belonging to an infinite cluster, every vertex would have probability zero, and countable subadditivity would exclude an infinite cluster anywhere. Hence the origin has positive connection probability. Wired domination and <a href="#crit:magnetization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:magnetization">[crit:magnetization]</a> give positive averaged plus magnetization at $s$. Monotonicity extends this conclusion to every $t>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### The physical inverse temperature and the correlation ratio

We finish by matching the parameter just identified to the original bonds $1+\varepsilon\xi_e$. For $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ define $$b_\pm(\beta,\varepsilon)
       =\log\sinh\bigl(2\beta(1\pm\varepsilon)\bigr).$$ The mean $(b_++b_-)/2$ is continuous and strictly increasing from $-\infty$ to $+\infty$ as $\beta$ runs through $(0,\infty)$. It has a unique zero $\beta_*(\varepsilon)$, characterized by <a id="crit:physical-equation"></a>$$\label{crit:physical-equation}
 \sinh\bigl(2\beta_*(\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon)\bigr)
 \sinh\bigl(2\beta_*(\varepsilon)(1-\varepsilon)\bigr)=1.$$ Continuity and strict monotonicity imply $\beta_*(\varepsilon)\to\beta_0$ as $\varepsilon\to0$. At this parameter put $$D_*(\varepsilon)=\tfrac12
       \bigl(b_+(\beta_*,\varepsilon)-b_-(\beta_*,\varepsilon)\bigr).$$ Then $D_*(\varepsilon)>0$ for $\varepsilon>0$ and $D_*(\varepsilon)\to0$. Choose $\varepsilon_0>0$ so that this value belongs to the permitted disorder interval whenever $0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_0$. At $\beta_*$ the physical model is exactly $(t,D)=(0,D_*)$. Proposition <a href="#crit:identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:identification">38</a> makes its magnetization zero; monotonicity gives the same conclusion below $\beta_*$.

<a id="crit:physical-beta"></a>
If $\beta>\beta_*$, both increments $b_\pm(\beta,\varepsilon)-b_\pm(\beta_*,\varepsilon)$ are strictly positive. Choose $s>0$, inside the parameter window, smaller than their minimum. With the same environment, the physical couplings at $\beta$ dominate those with log-sinh values $s+D_*\xi_e$. The latter model has positive averaged magnetization by Proposition <a href="#crit:identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:identification">38</a>. Consequently $$\label{crit:physical-beta}
 \beta_c(\varepsilon)=\beta_*(\varepsilon),
 \qquad m_\varepsilon(\beta_c(\varepsilon))=0.$$ This comparison keeps $D_*$ fixed; no assumption about holding the physical log-sinh difference constant while varying $\beta$ is used.

<details id="proof-stmt-crit-identification-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-crit-identification</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_0)$ and use $D=D_*(\varepsilon)>0$. By <a href="#crit:physical-beta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="crit:physical-beta">[crit:physical-beta]</a> and Proposition <a href="#crit:tuning-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="crit:tuning-zero">36</a>, the tuned free infinite-volume correlation of Proposition <a href="#read:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:moments">31</a> is $C_{\varepsilon,\omega}(r)$ at the physical critical inverse temperature. That proposition gives, at its stopping scale $J=\log r/\log L+O(1)$, $$r_J^{2d}\mathbb EC_{\varepsilon,\omega}(r)\asymp1,
 \qquad
 r_J^{4d}\mathbb EC_{\varepsilon,\omega}(r)^2=J^{1/4+o(1)}.$$ The factors $r_J^{4d}$ cancel in the ratio of the second moment to the square of the first. Thus $$\log R_\varepsilon(r)=(1/4+o(1))\log J+O(1),
 \qquad
 \frac{\log J}{\log\log r}\longrightarrow1,$$ which proves the asserted limit along all integer $r\to\infty$. The disorder remains fixed throughout this final limit. The thermodynamic limits and the separate Gibbs normalizations required in the definition of $R_\varepsilon$ are those already established in Proposition <a href="#read:moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="read:moments">31</a>. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two geometric mechanisms in the short-input estimate. In (a), the supports of the bad fields and the short input lie in the dotted square of radius $O(Mr)$. The middle solid square provides a proportional collar for pointwise normalization of the positive bad weight before averaging to the selected outer cut of radius $HR$. The ratio of these radii produces the $O(M/L)$ centered attenuation. In (b), an exceptional short-input contribution has a bad-center witness $z$ farther than $C_{0}Mr$ from its anchor $c_{i}$, but within the local decision window of size $O(Lr)$, where $C_{0}$ is the fixed witness-separation constant. The separated footprints include the disorder and all decision seeds, so the witness flag is independent of that input. The panels illustrate separate cases; no particular arrangement of bad fields or grid cells is prescribed.",
      "figure_id": "norm:geometry",
      "pdf_page": 25,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "norm:geometry",
          "exact_quote": "The two geometric mechanisms in the short-input estimate.\nIn (a), the supports of the bad fields and the short input lie in the\ndotted square of radius $O(Mr)$.  The middle solid square provides a\nproportional collar for pointwise\nnormaliza",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small,\n  outer/.style={draw=blue!55!black,line width=.8pt},\n  inner/.style={draw=blue!55!black,line width=.65pt},\n  footprint/.style={draw=black!65,densely dashed,line width=.65pt},\n  guide/.style={draw=black!45,densely dotted},\n  dimension/.style={draw=black!65,{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}}]\n  \\node[anchor=south,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (0,2.75)\n    {(a) Isolated bad component};\n  \\draw[outer] (-2.5,-2.5) rectangle (2.5,2.5);\n  \\fill[blue!5] (-1.65,-1.65) rectangle (1.65,1.65);\n  \\fill[white] (-1.05,-.9) rectangle (1.05,1.2);\n  \\draw[inner] (-1.65,-1.65) rectangle (1.65,1.65);\n  \\draw[guide] (-1.05,-.9) rectangle (1.05,1.2);\n  \\node[align=center] at (0,.35) {Bad supports\\\\and input $h_i$};\n  \\node[font=\\footnotesize] at (0,-.57) {radius $O(Mr)$};\n  \\node[fill=blue!5,inner sep=1.5pt] at (0,-1.27) {Proportional collar};\n  \\node[fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt] at (0,2.09)\n    {Outer cut: radius $HR$};\n  \\node[fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt] at (0,-2.1)\n    {Inner normalization square};\n  \\draw[black!65,-{Stealth[length=1.3mm]}] (0,-1.89) -- (0,-1.67);\n  \\node at (0,-2.90) {$R=Lr,\\qquad M\\ll L$};\n\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=7.25cm]\n    \\node[anchor=south,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (0,2.75)\n      {(b) A distant bad witness};\n    \\draw[guide] (-2.5,-2.5) rectangle (2.5,2.5);\n    \\node[fill=white,inner sep=1.5pt] at (0,2.08)\n      {Local decision window: $O(Lr)$};\n    \\draw[footprint,fill=blue!4] (-2.0,-.65) rectangle (-.7,.65);\n    \\fill (-1.35,0) circle[radius=1.5pt];\n    \\node[anchor=south] at (-1.35,.1) {$c_i$};\n    \\node[anchor=north,align=center] at (-1.35,-.77)\n      {Input footprint\\\\$n_i\\le M$};\n    \\draw[footprint,fill=orange!7] (.90,-.42) rectangle (1.74,.42);\n    \\fill[orange!65!black] (1.32,0) circle[radius=1.8pt];\n    \\node[anchor=west,inner sep=1pt] at (1.43,0) {$z$};\n    \\node[anchor=north,align=center] at (1.32,-.77)\n      {Flag footprint};\n    \\draw[dimension] (-1.35,1.1) -- (1.32,1.1)\n      node[midway,above,inner sep=2pt] {distance $>C_0Mr$};\n {Schematic; not to scale.};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two schematic diagrams labeled (a) and (b). Panel (a), titled \"(a) Isolated bad component\", shows three concentric squares: an outer solid square indicated by an arrow from the label \"Outer cut: radius HR\", an intermediate solid square indicated by an arrow from the label \"Proportional collar\" with \"Inner normalization square\" below, and an inner dotted square with light gray fill containing the text \"Bad supports and input $h_i$\" and \"radius $O(Mr)$\". Below panel (a) is the equation \"$R = Lr, \\quad M \\ll L$\". Panel (b), titled \"(b) A distant bad witness\", shows an outer solid square labeled above with \"Local decision window: $O(Lr)$\". Inside, on the left is a point $c_i$ in a dashed square labeled \"Input footprint\" and \"$n_i \\le M$\"; on the right is a point $z$ in a dashed square labeled \"Flag footprint\". A horizontal double-headed arrow between them is labeled \"distance $> C_0 M r$\". Below panel (b) is the text \"Independent randomness\". At the bottom right is the note \"Schematic; not to scale.\""
    },
    {
      "caption": "Geometry of the terminal readout. The solid inner squares are the reference contours defining $w_{J, ya}$, centered at the cells $c_{J}(y_{a})$. The dashed principal footprints record the disorder and decision seeds determining $α̃_{J, ya}$; their disjointness, including the safety layer for edge variables, gives independence of the two amplitudes. The dotted enclosure has bounded diameter in terminal cell units. A surrounding square provides margin of order $br_{J}$, where $b = J^{4/25}$. Removing terminal interactions whose spin supports meet this square leaves a reference conditional expectation inside it. The two spin observables are still evaluated jointly in that expectation. Shapes and relative lengths are schematic.",
      "figure_id": "read:geometry",
      "pdf_page": 51,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "read:geometry",

          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],

width=.7pt},\n  guide/.style={draw=black!45,densely dotted},\n  dimension/.style={draw=black!65,{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]\n  \\fill[black!3] (-3.15,-3.15) rectangle (3.15,3.15);\n  \\fill[white] (-2.60,-1.5) rectangle (2.60,1.5);\n  \\draw[black!65,line width=.8pt] (-3.15,-3.15) rectangle (3.15,3.15);\n  \\node[anchor=south] at (0,3.28) {Terminal buffer};\n  \\draw[guide] (-2.60,-1.5) rectangle (2.60,1.5);\n\n  \\foreach \\cx in {-1.5,1.5} {\n    \\draw[footprint,fill=blue!3] (\\cx-1,-1) rectangle (\\cx+1,1);\n    \\draw[contour] (\\cx-.45,-.45) rectangle (\\cx+.45,.45);\n  }\n  \\fill (-1.41,.05) circle[radius=1.5pt];\n  \\fill (1.59,.05) circle[radius=1.5pt];\n  \\node[anchor=north] at (-1.41,-.03) {$y_1$};\n  \\node[anchor=north] at (1.59,-.03) {$y_2$};\n  \\node[anchor=south] at (-1.5,.50) {$w_{J,y_1}$};\n  \\node[anchor=south] at (1.5,.50) {$w_{J,y_2}$};\n  \\node[anchor=north] at (-1.5,-1.05) {$\\widetilde\\alpha_{J,y_1}$};\n  \\node[anchor=north] at (1.5,-1.05) {$\\widetilde\\alpha_{J,y_2}$};\n\n  \\draw[dimension] (2.80,1.5) -- (2.80,3.15)\n    node[midway,left,fill=black!3,inner sep=2pt] {$b r_J$};\n  \\draw[guide] (2.60,1.5) -- (2.97,1.5);\n  \\draw[guide] (-1.41,-1.55) -- (-1.41,-2.08);\n  \\draw[guide] (1.59,-1.55) -- (1.59,-2.08);\n  \\draw[dimension] (-1.41,-1.92) -- (1.59,-1.92)\n    node[midway,below,fill=black!3,inner sep=2pt] {$\\ell$};\n  \\node at (0,-2.65) {$F\\le \\ell/r_J<LF$};\n\n  \\draw[contour] (3.60,1.62) -- (4.12,1.62);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=3.15cm] at (4.25,1.62)\n    {Reference contours\\\\supporting $w_{J,y_a}$};\n  \\draw[footprint] (3.60,.35) -- (4.12,.35);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=3.15cm] at (4.25,.35)\n    {Random footprints\\\\of the principal slots};\n  \\draw[guide] (3.60,-.95) -- (4.12,-.95);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=3.15cm] at (4.25,-.95)\n    {A bounded insertion\\\\enclosure};\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=3.7cm,font=\\footnotesize]\n    at (3.60,-2.45) {Schematic; not to scale.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic geometric diagram showing an outer solid square labeled at the top \"Terminal buffer\". Inside, separated from the outer boundary by a margin labeled \"$br_J$\" with dimension arrows at the upper right, is a dotted rectangular enclosure. Inside this enclosure are two configurations separated horizontally by a dimension line labeled \"$\\ell$\", with the relation \"$F \\le \\ell / r_J < L F$\" printed below. The left configuration consists of a center point $y_1$, an inner solid square labeled \"$w_{J,y_1}$\", and an enclosing dashed square labeled \"$\\widetilde{\\alpha}_{J,y_1}$\". The right configuration consists of a center point $y_2$, an inner solid square labeled \"$w_{J,y_2}$\", and an enclosing dashed square labeled \"$\\widetilde{\\alpha}_{J,y_2}$\". To the right of the main diagram is a legend with three entries: a solid horizontal line labeled \"Reference contours supporting $w_{J,y_a}$\", a dashed horizontal line labeled \"Random footprints of the principal slots\", and a dotted horizontal line labeled \"A bounded insertion enclosure\", followed below by \"Schematic; not to scale.\""
    }

      "explanation": "The main theorem assumes the deterministic reference-model estimates of the conformal universality result.",
      "relation": "uses",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-218.3",
      "source_quote": "Assume the deterministic reference-model estimates of [[ReferenceIsing](#ref-ReferenceIsing)], at the scopes stated in Section <a href=\"#sec:reference\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:reference\">2</a>.",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-model-main",

      "basis": "inferred",

      "source_anchor": "stmt-ref-comparison",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-218.3",
      "source_quote": "Either law can be replaced by a mixture;",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-in-comparison",

      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",

      "explanation": "The relative fluctuation analysis assumes deterministic critical reference estimates derived from boundary laws in reference geometries.",

      "source_pm_id": "PM-218.3",
      "source_quote": "Assume the deterministic reference-model estimates of",

      "target_quote": "use the critical plus-boundary law in a larger reference square.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The ratio bound on conditional spin configurations in separated regions of the critical reference model relies directly on the random cluster comparison theorem with deleted pins.",

      "source_anchor": "stmt-ref-ope",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-218.3",
      "source_quote": "Consider a finite critical reference graph in a square chart. Let $I,J$ and $V_{\\mathrm{pin}}$ be pairwise disjoint vertex sets",
